- reputation_1861 unknown: Welles says he presented himself to the Department once, about his Pacific orders (WELLES-D1-p20-21); nothing on public standing
- boastful: 'impressed with and boastful of his own powers, given to exaggeration in relation to himself' (WELLES-D-1862-10-01) [Welles's view]
- ambitious: 'excessive and sometimes not over-scrupulous ambition' (WELLES-D-1862-10-01) [Welles's view]
- disdains_superiors: 'not generous to older and superior living officers, whom he is too ready to traduce' (WELLES-D-1862-10-01) [Welles's view; Welles also reports Farragut and Davis charged W. D. Porter, not him]
- intrigues: Welles judged that Porter, through Seward, urged Barron's substitution for Stringham as detail officer in April 1861 (WELLES-D1-p20-21) [Welles's view; contested; single source]
- Welles records Lincoln ordering the Powhatan restored to the Sumter expedition on the night of 5 Apr 1861 (WELLES-D1-p24-25); Porter's command of her toward Pickens is in APPLETONS-porter_david_d; not tagged, see NOTE-march-1861-facts
- proposed tag: none

#### stringham_silas_h: Silas H. Stringham
- competence above: at Hatteras Inlet (26-29 Aug 1861) he kept his ships under way in an ellipse and took both forts; Hawkins calls it 'admirable skill, worthy of a great commander' and quotes Boynton that the manoeuvre was first tried in the navy there (BL-1-p633-634) [participant memoir: Hawkins commanded troops in the expedition]; Congress thanked him 7 Feb 1863 (CALLAHAN-THANKS; outside window); never again held a sea command (BL-1-p634)
- nerve average: Welles's retrospective says Stringham and Ward first thought two fast tugs might relieve Sumter and Stringham 'came ultimately but reluctantly' to the opposing view, and that he doubted the practicability of leading the expedition (WELLES-D1-p8-9; WELLES-D1-p15) [Welles's view, written later; contested: Appletons says he strongly urged relief, APPLETONS-stringham_silas_h]; he declined further active service on account of age (APPLETONS-stringham_silas_h) [single source]
- experience high: midshipman 15 Nov 1809, lieutenant 9 Dec 1814, commander 3 Mar 1831, captain 8 Sep 1841 (CALLAHAN-stringham_silas_h); Algerine war, Vera Cruz, three yard commands, Mediterranean squadron 1853-56 (APPLETONS-stringham_silas_h)
- reputation_1861 above: Welles, with Lincoln's concurrence, chose him 'to assist me in matters of detail' because he knew him faithful (WELLES-D1-p5-6) [Welles's view; single source]
- Lincoln's memorandum of 1 Apr 1861, as printed by Welles, sends Stringham to Pensacola and has Barron relieve him in the Bureau of Detail; Welles resisted it (WELLES-D1-p16-17; WELLES-D1-p20-21) [Welles's view]
- Welles, 3 Dec 1862, says Stringham chaired the yard board and showed a partiality for New London (WELLES-D-1862-12-03) [Welles's view]; not tagged

#### wilkes_charles: Charles Wilkes
- competence average: stopped the Trent on 8 Nov 1861 and Congress passed thanks (APPLETONS-wilkes_charles); Seward conceded the act was lawful in substance but that he should have taken the ship to a prize court (APPLETONS-wilkes_charles). Welles, 10 Aug 1862: 'abilities but not good judgment in all respects' (WELLES-D-1862-08-10) [Welles's view]; on 10 Sep 1862 Wilkes was left on the upper James without orders or army (WELLES-D-1862-09-10)
- nerve high: Fairfax, his executive officer, says he decided to intercept the Trent, cruised to the Bahama Channel and took her alone (BL-2-p135) [participant memoir; Wilkes's subordinate]
- experience high: midshipman 1 Jan 1818, lieutenant 28 Apr 1826, commander 13 Jul 1843, captain 14 Sep 1855 (CALLAHAN-wilkes_charles); commanded the Exploring Expedition squadron 1838-42 (APPLETONS-wilkes_charles)
- reputation_1861 above: gold medal of the Geographical Society of London for the expedition; also a court-martial in 1842 for illegally punishing crew, reprimanded (APPLETONS-wilkes_charles) [single source]
- insubordinate: Welles, Aug 1862, wrote to him that he 'interposes his own authority to interrupt the execution of the orders of the Department' and wished to dismiss officers at will (WELLES-D-1862-08-10); the Trent seizure had no orders from Washington (BL-2-p135) [Welles's view; participant]
- rank_proud: Welles says he was 'unwilling to report to Goldsborough' (WELLES-D-1862-08-10) [Welles's view; single source]
- Welles, 11 Sep 1862: 'likes to exhibit himself'; Welles pressed him to sail with his West India squadron (WELLES-D-1862-09-11; WELLES-D-1862-09-16); not tagged

#### davis_charles_h: Charles H. Davis
- competence above: secretary of the officers' conference of 1861 (BL-1-p671); fleet captain at Port Royal (APPLETONS-davis_charles_h); as flag officer the Union flotilla with Ellet's rams captured four and destroyed three of eight Confederate vessels at Memphis on 6 Jun 1862 (BL-1-p627; APPLETONS-davis_charles_h; BL-1-p454-457); thanks of Congress 7 Feb 1863 cite Fort Pillow and Memphis (CALLAHAN-THANKS; outside window). Welles, 1 Oct 1862: 'an intelligent but not an energetic, driving, fighting officer' (WELLES-D-1862-10-01) [Welles's view]
- nerve below: Ellet says Davis did not approve passing Fort Pillow though he let Ellet try it (BL-1-p454-457) [rival memoir: Alfred Ellet, army ram fleet]; Welles, 10 Aug 1862, says the two flag officers abandoned Vicksburg and Davis went up river (WELLES-D-1862-08-10) [Welles's view; contested]; Davis's report explains the limits of his steam power (BL-1-p448)
- experience above: midshipman 12 Aug 1823, lieutenant 3 Mar 1831, commander 12 Jun 1854 (CALLAHAN-davis_charles_h); coast survey, Nautical Almanac, sloop St. Mary's (APPLETONS-davis_charles_h); first flag command 1862
- reputation_1861 above: his Nantucket survey brought thanks from insurers and merchants and the Almanac was largely his work (APPLETONS-davis_charles_h) [single source]

#### dahlgren_john_a_b: John A. Dahlgren
- competence high: designed the gun that formed the navy's armament and built up the Washington ordnance establishment (APPLETONS-dahlgren_john_a_b); Congress thanked him on 7 Feb 1863 for 'improvements in ordnance, and zealous and efficient labors in the ordnance branch' (CALLAHAN-THANKS; outside window); Welles, 9 Oct 1862: 'I appreciate Dahlgren' but would not give him Charleston (WELLES-D-1862-10-01; WELLES-D-1862-10-09) [Welles's view]
- nerve average: Welles's retrospective calls Dahlgren and Meigs 'cautious, not to say timid' in the Merrimac panic of March 1862 and says Dahlgren could give the President no advice (WELLES-D1-p62-63) [Welles's view, written later; contested]; Appletons calls him a man of great personal bravery (APPLETONS-dahlgren_john_a_b) [single source]
- experience above: passed midshipman 28 Apr 1832, lieutenant 8 Mar 1837, commander 14 Sep 1855 (CALLAHAN-dahlgren_john_a_b); ordnance duty from 1847, gun first cast 1850, Plymouth experimental cruise 1857-59 (APPLETONS-dahlgren_john_a_b)
- reputation_1861 above: 'the world's recognition as a man of science and inventive genius' (APPLETONS-dahlgren_john_a_b) [single source; written later]
- organiser: ordnance foundry, carriage shops and experimental battery at Washington under his direction (APPLETONS-dahlgren_john_a_b)
- ambitious: Welles, 1 and 9 Oct 1862: his 'ambition is great', asked for Charleston and seeks the highest honours (WELLES-D-1862-10-01; WELLES-D-1862-10-09) [Welles's view]
- political_patron: Welles records the President's partiality and his willingness to give Dahlgren the highest grade if Welles wrote a letter (WELLES-D-1862-10-09) [Welles's view]; sponsor Lincoln
- Welles also says Dahlgren worked on a War Department order to load boats without Navy Department authority in March 1862 (WELLES-D1-p62-63); not tagged

#### rodgers_john: John Rodgers
- competence high: bought and converted the first river gunboats in May 1861 under the War Department (BL-1-p620; BL-1-p358); at Drewry's Bluff on 15 May 1862 he anchored the Galena under the fort's guns, quoted as 'one of the most masterly pieces of seamanship of the whole war', and the attack showed the fort could not be reduced without the army (BL-2-p268-270) [Soley quotes an officer in the fort]; McClellan relied on his opinion on the river and the Harrison's Bar position (MCCLELLAN-OS-p429-430; MCCLELLAN-OS-p437) [own memoir: McClellan]; Irwin calls him 'most gallant and able' (BL-2-p172) [participant memoir: McClellan's staff]
- nerve high: knew the Galena's light armour 'would be riddled' yet 'resolved to give the matter a fair trial' (BL-2-p268-270) [quoted private letter]
- experience above: midshipman 18 Apr 1828, passed midshipman 14 Jun 1834, lieutenant 28 Jan 1840, commander 14 Sep 1855 (CALLAHAN-rodgers_john); Florida coast survey, North Pacific exploring expedition 1853-56 (APPLETONS-rodgers_john); commander only in 1861
- reputation_1861 unknown: nothing contemporary found
- organiser: purchased and altered the Tyler, Lexington and Conestoga into gunboats and handed the station to Foote on 12 Sep 1861 (BL-1-p358; BL-1-p360; BL-1-p620)
- candid: private letter admitting his doubt of the Galena (BL-2-p268-270)
- joint_minded: McClellan's telegram of July 1862 says Rodgers 'is doing all in his power in the kindest and most efficient manner' (MCCLELLAN-OS-p485) [own memoir: McClellan]
- Conflict: Appletons gives 129 hits on the Galena and heavy crew losses; Soley gives 28 hits, 18 perforations (APPLETONS-rodgers_john; BL-2-p268-270); Soley used

#### buchanan_franklin: Franklin Buchanan
- competence above: commanded the Virginia on 8 Mar 1862, sinking the Cumberland and forcing the Congress to surrender (BL-1-p700-701); Wood calls him 'energetic and high-toned ... daring courage ... great professional ability' (BL-1-p694-695) [participant memoir: Wood served under him; admiring]
- nerve high: wounded in the first day's fight and sent to hospital (BL-1-p700-701); Wood says Tattnall found him 'desperately wounded' with his crew nearly all killed or disabled (BL-1-p705-706) [participant memoir]
- experience high: midshipman 28 Jan 1815, lieutenant 13 Jan 1825, commander 8 Sep 1841, captain 14 Sep 1855 (CALLAHAN-buchanan_franklin); first Naval Academy superintendent 1845, Susquehanna in Perry's Japan expedition 1852, Washington Navy Yard from 1859 (APPLETONS-buchanan_franklin)
- Wood adds that Buchanan was to be promoted to admiral and retired from command by his wound (BL-1-p704)
- reputation_1861 above: selected to organise the Naval Academy and captained Perry's flagship (APPLETONS-buchanan_franklin) [single source]
- organiser: Wood says he made every exertion to hasten the fitting out of the Virginia; his Academy organisation in 1845 (BL-1-p694-695; APPLETONS-buchanan_franklin)
- Neutral record: he resigned on 22 Apr 1861, asked to withdraw it and was refused; Callahan records dismissal on 22 May 1861 (APPLETONS-buchanan_franklin; CALLAHAN-buchanan_franklin) [conflict on resignation versus dismissal]

#### tattnall_josiah: Josiah Tattnall
- competence average: his small steamers sortied at Port Royal on 5 and 6 Nov 1861 and retired, the Savannah firing 'somewhat wildly' (BL-1-p677-678) [participant memoir: Ammen, Union side]; he commanded the Virginia from 29 Mar 1862 and burned her on 11 May after Norfolk fell, then won an honourable acquittal on 5 Jul 1862 (BL-1-p705-706; BL-1-p710-711; BL-2-p268; APPLETONS-tattnall_josiah) [participant memoir: Wood, defending him; Appletons also records an earlier court of inquiry censure, contested]
- nerve average: Wood says he pulled through heavy fire to the wounded Buchanan's ship ('Blood is thicker than water') (BL-1-p705-706) [participant memoir]; before Norfolk fell he asked Mallory for leave to pass the forts (BL-1-p707); on 10-11 May he tried to lighten her to run up the James, the pilots refused, and he burned her (BL-1-p709-711) [participant memoir; contested]
- experience high: midshipman 1 Jan 1812, lieutenant 1 Apr 1818, commander 25 Feb 1838, captain 5 Feb 1850 (CALLAHAN-tattnall_josiah); Mexican War, flag officer of the Asiatic station 1857-59 (APPLETONS-tattnall_josiah)
- reputation_1861 above: Georgia's thanks and a sword after Vera Cruz, public sympathy for the Peiho episode (APPLETONS-tattnall_josiah) [single source]
- slaveholder: not tagged, needs-source (no slave schedule read)

#### semmes_raphael: Raphael Semmes
- competence high: fitted out the Sumter at New Orleans, ran the blockade on 18 Jun 1861 and, per Soley as quoted in a B&L footnote, made 17 prizes and burned six vessels (BL-2-p135) [Union naval writer, quoted; single source]; then took the Alabama (APPLETONS-semmes_raphael) [single source for the Alabama's later record, outside window]
- nerve above: ran the blockade out of New Orleans with the Sumter on 18 Jun 1861 (BL-2-p135) [single source]
- experience above: midshipman 1 Apr 1826, passed midshipman 28 Apr 1832, lieutenant 9 Feb 1837, commander 14 Sep 1855 (CALLAHAN-semmes_raphael); Mexican War flag lieutenant, lost the Somers, light-house board secretary (APPLETONS-semmes_raphael)
- reputation_1861 unknown: nothing contemporary found for March 1861
- Bulloch says the Navy Department ordered Semmes to England from Nassau to take the Alabama (BULLOCH-SS-p233-234) [own memoir: Bulloch]; Semmes's own books were not used
- Neutral record: resigned 15 Feb 1861 and spent three weeks buying munitions in the North for the Confederacy (APPLETONS-semmes_raphael; CALLAHAN-semmes_raphael); slaveholder: needs-source, no slave schedule read

#### bulloch_james_d: James D. Bulloch
- competence above: contracted the Alabama with Laird for GBP 47,500 and sent her out on 29 Jul 1862 before the law officers' report was acted on (BULLOCH-SS-p62-63; BULLOCH-SS-p260-261) [own memoir; single source]; Mallory's despatches leave 'all details' of the French ship plan to his judgment (BULLOCH-SS-p397-401) [own memoir quoting Mallory]
- nerve above: took the Fingal into Savannah through the blockade in Nov 1861 (BULLOCH-SS-p379-380) [own memoir; single source]
- experience above: midshipman 21 Jun 1839, passed midshipman 2 Jul 1845, lieutenant 18 Oct 1853, resigned 5 Oct 1854 (CALLAHAN-bulloch_james_d); had superintended construction of vessels before the war (BULLOCH-SS-p62-63) [own memoir]
- reputation_1861 unknown: no contemporary judgement found; the memoir shows the Navy Department expecting him at Montgomery (BULLOCH-SS-p41-42)
- Searched: Appletons 1887-89 (no entry), Wright 1911 (naval officers not covered). Neutral and rival views not read; ORN Series II volume 1 could not be loaded.

### Members of Congress (`congress`)

Stat directions are for hidden-stat centres, not numbers. Page numbers from OCR headers can be off by one; keys with `-ch` give a chapter. Counts of JCCW questions are my own derivation from OCR (approximate).

#### wade_benjamin_f: Benjamin F. Wade
- competence above: Chaired Territories (CONGDIR37-2-p23) and the JCCW (signs the Bull Run report, JCCW-1863-2-p8). With Chandler and Trumbull he called on Lincoln on 26 and 28 Oct 1861 to press for an advance (NH-4-p467), and as chairman he gave the 6 Jan 1862 cabinet conference a "bold and vigorous speech" (JULIAN-PR-p203) [own memoir: Julian, a JCCW colleague]. Julian, who sat with him on the committee, called him "a man of uncommon downrightness" (JULIAN-PR-p356) [own memoir]. Riddle calls him "first man in the senate" (RIDDLE-WADE-p240) and credits him with pushing Stanton (RIDDLE-WADE-p248, via Senator Pearce) [single source; admiring biography by his friend]. Nicolay and Hay judge the committee "often hasty and unjust" but sustained by Congress (NH-5-p150-151). Held at above, not high: the committee needed about two months to move Lincoln on army corps (JULIAN-PR-p205).
- nerve high: At Bull Run on 21 July 1861 he armed himself and tried to halt fleeing troops (RIDDLE-WADE-p244-245) [single source; partly a quoted letter by an unnamed member of the party]. Told McClellan an unsuccessful battle was better than delay (NH-4-p467). Montgomery Blair called him "earnest, vigorous, sincere" (WELLES-D-1862-09-12, Welles recording Blair's words). Voted for the July 1861 Crittenden-Johnson resolution without speaking (RIDDLE-WADE-p242).
- experience high: Senate from 15 March 1851 (ten years); prosecuting attorney, state senator, president judge (BDAC-wade_benjamin_f).
- reputation_1861 above: Riddle says "Old Ben Wade" had "already" become his name (RIDDLE-WADE-p241) and ranks him next to Stevens as a congressional leader of the war (RIDDLE-WADE-p241); re-elected to the Senate twice (BDAC-wade_benjamin_f; OCR digits garbled) [single source, retrospective].
- antislavery: Riddle says the Wade-Stevens-Stanton group said "the conservative notion of preserving the constitution, as next to slavery, the thing not to be touched, always provoked their derision" and that Wade saw the real objective as abolition (RIDDLE-WADE-p241, RIDDLE-WADE-p243) [single source].
- Conflict: Riddle (RIDDLE-WADE-p247, footnote) "thinks" Johnson never acted on the committee; the testimony shows him questioning from 26 Dec 1861 (JCCW-1863-2-p52-54, see johnson_andrew).
- Not tagged: intrigues. The Oct 1861 effort to have Scott retired (MCCLELLAN-OS-p171, McClellan's own letter [rival memoir: McClellan]) is lobbying of the executive, not a plot against a colleague; see proposed tag presses_command.
- Date check: BDAC dates read with OCR corrections (born 27 Oct 1800 agrees with Appletons).

#### chandler_zachariah: Zachariah Chandler
- competence above: Brought forward the Senate resolution for an inquiry after Ball's Bluff, which led to the JCCW (RIDDLE-WADE-p246; NH-5-p150-151, which says the Senate adopted Grimes's motion for a joint committee instead). Riddle writes "Wade and Chandler were the two great men of it" (RIDDLE-WADE-p247 footnote) [single source]. Asked 128 questions in the Bull Run and Ball's Bluff volume (the most of any named member there) and 45 in the Western volume (JCCW-1863-2, JCCW-1863-3; derived OCR count, approximate).
- nerve high: Wrote on 10 Feb 1861 that "without a little blood-letting, this Union will not, in my estimation, be worth a rush" and asked for "stiff-backed men" at the Peace Convention (CHANDLER-OS-p190-191) [his own letter, printed in a partisan 1880 sketch]. In a Senate speech of early 1861 he called for enforcing the laws against secession (CHANDLER-OS-p191-192). Rode out to Bull Run with Wade (RIDDLE-WADE-p244-245). Julian groups him with Sumner and Wade as willing to save the Union at all hazards (JULIAN-PR-p356) [own memoir].
- experience average: Mayor of Detroit 1851; Senator from early 1857 (CHANDLER-OS-p49-50; BDAC-chandler_zachariah, whose first election digits read 1867 by OCR error). About four years in the Senate by March 1861.
- reputation_1861 unknown: Searched BDAC, Chandler sketch, Riddle, Nicolay and Hay 3-6 for a March 1861 judgement of his national standing; only retrospective grouping with Wade found (RIDDLE-WADE-p239).
- Chairman of Commerce on the July 1861 list (CONGDIR37-2-p22). Voted for the July 1861 resolution without speaking (RIDDLE-WADE-p242).
- Proposed tag: presses_command (see report). Visits to Lincoln 26 and 28 Oct 1861 (NH-4-p467) and to McClellan (MCCLELLAN-OS-p171).

#### johnson_andrew: Andrew Johnson
- competence above: Sat on Commerce, Public Lands, Territories and other Senate committees (CONGDIR37-2-p22, CONGDIR37-2-p23). Offered the resolution in the Senate on 25 July 1861 that mirrored Crittenden's war-aims resolution; it passed with five dissenting votes (NH-4-p379-380). Named to the JCCW (NH-5-p150-151; JULIAN-PR-p201). Lincoln made him military governor with a brigadier's rank on 4 March 1862 (BDAC-johnson_andrew; HEIT1-johnson_andrew).
- nerve high: Stayed and fought secession in the Senate while Tennessee left the Union; Nicolay and Hay cite his "devoted loyalty" and "energetic defense of the Union" (NH-4-p368; NH-5-ch4). A correspondent of Lincoln's praised his Dec 1860 declaration that wrongs must be settled inside the Union (NH-3-p256) [writer unnamed in the passage].
- experience high: Representative 1843-53; Governor 1853-57; Senator from Oct 1857 (BDAC-johnson_andrew).
- reputation_1861 above: The Dec 1860 speech above (NH-3-p256); Nicolay and Hay name him as a conspicuous example of Southern loyalty in the Senate (NH-4-p368). Retrospective authors.
- JCCW: questioned witnesses from 26 Dec 1861 (JCCW-1863-2-p52-54; first recorded question to McDowell) and in Jan 1862 (JCCW-1863-2); 11 questions in Part 2 and about 10 entries in Part 3 by derived OCR count (JCCW-1863-2, JCCW-1863-3); the later Part 3 hits include a June 1862 hearing, after he left, so they are not his. Riddle's claim that he never acted (RIDDLE-WADE-p247) is contradicted [conflict].
- Not tagged: slaveholder (needs-source: census slave schedule); no antislavery or press tag documented in the sources read.

#### julian_george_w: George W. Julian
- competence average: Member of the JCCW (JULIAN-PR-p201; NH-5-p150-151) and a regular attender, with the committee at the 6 Jan 1862 conference with the cabinet (JULIAN-PR-p203; "from five to six hours per day", JULIAN-PR-p204 [own memoir]). Asked 11 questions in Part 2 and 47 in Part 3 (JCCW-1863-2, JCCW-1863-3; derived OCR count). Raised the issue of Cameron's return of a fugitive girl in a House debate in Dec 1861 (JULIAN-PR-p200) [own memoir, single source]. No evidence of moving bills or colleagues beyond that in 1861-62.
- nerve above: Left the Whigs for the Free-Soil party in 1848 and ran for Vice President on the Free-Soil ticket in 1852 (BDAC-julian_george_w); in Dec 1861 attacked a cabinet officer's sincerity on the floor (JULIAN-PR-p200) [own memoir].
- experience below: One earlier term (1849-51) and one state-house term (1845); elected again in 1860 (BDAC-julian_george_w).
- reputation_1861 average: Free-Soil nominee for Vice President in 1852 and chair of the organization committee at the 1856 Republican convention (BDAC-julian_george_w); no wider contemporary judgement found.
- antislavery: Free-Soil delegate and elector 1848 (BDAC-julian_george_w); his account says the JCCW "had its birth in the popular demand ... for less tenderness toward slavery" (JULIAN-PR-p201) [own memoir].
- Memoir caution [own memoir]: he writes that the majority of the committee were "morbidly sensitive" and "practically incapable" of doing McClellan justice and suspected him of treason (JULIAN-PR-p204, JULIAN-PR-p205). Appointment date: he gives 19 Dec 1861 (JULIAN-PR-p201); other sources 9-10 Dec for creation (needs-source: Congressional Globe, 37th Congress, 2nd session).
- Birth date conflict: BDAC 6 May 1817; Appletons 5 May 1817 (APPLETONS-julian_george_w).

#### covode_john: John Covode
- competence above: Welles, on 9 Jan 1863, wrote that Covode "is shrewd but illiterate, a match and more than a match for men of higher culture" (WELLES-D-1863-01-09) [hostile-leaning diarist, single source for the judgement]. Appletons says he made "a national reputation by his vigor and penetration as chairman of the special committee" that investigated charges against President Buchanan in 1860, with a report printed by order of Congress (APPLETONS-covode_john) [single source; needs the 1860 House report]. 59 questions in Part 2 and 58 in Part 3 on the JCCW (JCCW-1863-2, JCCW-1863-3; derived OCR count).
- nerve unknown: Searched BDAC, Appletons, JCCW volumes, Julian, Nicolay and Hay 3-6, Welles; no documented act of political risk.
- experience average: Representative from 4 March 1855, about six years by March 1861 (BDAC-covode_john), plus two terms in the legislature (Appletons).
- reputation_1861 above: The 1860 committee report "attracted much attention" (APPLETONS-covode_john) [single source].
- ambitious: told Welles on 9 Jan 1863 he expected to be a candidate for Governor of Pennsylvania (WELLES-D-1863-01-09).
- JCCW member (NH-5-p150-151; JULIAN-PR-p201). Appletons calls him "an active member" and says he "earnestly supported" Lincoln (APPLETONS-covode_john).
- Birth date conflict: BDAC 18 March 1808; Appletons 17 March 1808.

#### gooch_daniel_w: Daniel W. Gooch
- competence unknown: Searched BDAC, JCCW Parts 2 and 3, Julian, Nicolay and Hay 3-6, Welles (July 1863: Welles complains that Gooch and Hooper want to run the Boston navy yard for party ends, WELLES-D-1863-07-16; hostile, not a judgement of skill). Asked 108 questions in Part 2 and 119 in Part 3 (JCCW-1863-2, JCCW-1863-3; derived OCR count), which shows diligence, not skill.
- nerve unknown: no documented political risk found.
- experience below: In Congress from 31 Jan 1858 (about three years by March 1861); Massachusetts House 1852 and convention 1853 (BDAC-gooch_daniel_w, OCR digits corrected from context).
- reputation_1861 unknown: no judgement found.
- JCCW member (NH-5-p150-151; JULIAN-PR-p201).

#### odell_moses_f: Moses F. Odell
- competence unknown: Searched BDAC, JCCW volumes, Julian, Nicolay and Hay 3-6, Welles (12 Mar 1864: Odell and Gooch report seized papers showing contract fraud, WELLES-D-1864-03-12; no judgement of him). 57 questions in Part 2 and 50 in Part 3 (JCCW-1863-2, JCCW-1863-3; derived OCR count).
- nerve unknown: a Democrat who stayed on a Republican-run committee, but no documented act of risk.
- experience below: first elected in 1860; before that a New York custom-house clerk and appraiser from 1846 (BDAC-odell_moses_f).
- reputation_1861 unknown: no judgement found.
- JCCW member and one of two Democrats (NH-5-p150-151; JULIAN-PR-p201).

#### stevens_thaddeus: Thaddeus Stevens
- competence high: Leader of the House as chairman of Ways and Means, which carried both finance and appropriations (WOODBURN-STEVENS-p239; first-listed on the Dec 1861 committee list, CONGDIR37-2-p24-26). He took the appropriation bills and left currency and loans to Spaulding and taxes to Morrill (WOODBURN-STEVENS-p250-251). He pressed the Legal Tender bill, which passed after Senate amendments he disliked (WOODBURN-STEVENS-p263, WOODBURN-STEVENS-p277-278). Chase wrote to him on 29 Jan 1862 about the clause (NH-6-p232). Julian: "no possible emergency could disconcert him" in debate (JULIAN-PR-p361) [own memoir; a colleague].
- nerve high: Julian says he made his thrusts at "unrighteous copperheads and self-righteous Republicans" alike and was "fifteen years in advance of his party" (JULIAN-PR-p361). Woodburn quotes his sneer at the Cabinet before the war began (WOODBURN-STEVENS-p239). Riddle puts him with Wade and Stanton as "revolutionary" (RIDDLE-WADE-p241) [admiring biography].
- experience high: State legislator 1833-41, canal commissioner, Representative 1849-53 and from 1859 (BDAC-stevens_thaddeus; OCR digits partly corrected).
- reputation_1861 above: Riddle calls him "the popular congressional idol of the war" (RIDDLE-WADE-p241, retrospective) [single source]; Woodburn says Lincoln considered him for Attorney General (WOODBURN-STEVENS-p239) [single source, no document named].
- antislavery: Woodburn's chapter on his anti-slavery speeches in the 1850s (WOODBURN-STEVENS-p73-123); Julian (JULIAN-PR-p361).
- ambitious: Wanted a Cabinet seat in 1861 and criticised the Cabinet when passed over (WOODBURN-STEVENS-p239) [single source].
- quarrelsome: "When he lost his temper ... his invective was absolutely remorseless" (JULIAN-PR-p362) [own memoir by colleague]; on 22 Jan 1864 he told Blair his speech "contained the distilled virus of the Copperhead" (WOODBURN-STEVENS-p226-227; outside the window).

#### sumner_charles: Charles Sumner
- competence above: Chairman of Foreign Relations (CONGDIR37-2-p22). The Cabinet invited him in on 25 Dec 1861 to read letters from Bright and Cobden on the Trent affair (NH-5-p35). His 9 Jan 1862 Trent speech was approved by Dr. Woolsey in a published letter of 11 Jan (PIERCE-SUMNER-p55-56) [Pierce is Sumner's friend and biographer; Woolsey's approval second-hand]. A Senate colleague calls him "the purest man in the Senate, if not the ablest" (JULIAN-PR-p356) [own memoir]. On the Legal Tender bill he made an "eloquent and impassioned speech" on its evils (NH-6-p233-234); on the July 1861 resolution he spoke but did not vote (RIDDLE-WADE-p242). Against: his Dec 1862 attack on Seward did not succeed (NH-6-p266-267), and the 1862 re-election needed a contested canvass (PIERCE-SUMNER-p98-99).
- nerve high: Struck down at his desk in 1856 and absent until Dec 1859 (BDAC-sumner_charles); in Oct 1861 spoke at Worcester for emancipation while the administration held back (PIERCE-SUMNER-p42-43) [friend's biography]. With Grimes and Trumbull he spoke "decidedly against" keeping Seward at the 19 Dec 1862 meeting and was "unrelenting and unforgiving" (WELLES-D-1862-12-20; NH-6-p266-267).
- experience high: Senator from April 1851, ten years (BDAC-sumner_charles; OCR prints 1861, corrected from context).
- reputation_1861 high: Free-Soil founder, assault by Brooks, three Senate elections (BDAC-sumner_charles); Pierce: "stands almost alone as a public man whose great support was the moral enthusiasm of the people" (PIERCE-SUMNER-p100) [friend's biography, retrospective].
- antislavery: Free-Soil founder 1848 (BDAC-sumner_charles); Worcester speech Oct 1861 (PIERCE-SUMNER-p42-43).
- Not tagged: intrigues. The Dec 1862 Senate caucus action against Seward was reported to Lincoln openly through a committee (WELLES-D-1862-12-19).

#### fessenden_william_p: William P. Fessenden
- competence high: Chairman of Finance on the 6 July 1861 list (CONGDIR37-2-p22). Opened the Senate debate on the Legal Tender clause in Feb 1862, putting it on "absolute, overwhelming necessity" (NH-6-p233-234). Julian: "majesty in the figure and brow ... sarcasm as keen as it was inimitable ... integrity perfect" (JULIAN-PR-p356) [own memoir by a colleague, a Republican radical describing a conservative]. Welles called him "skillful but a little tart" on 20 Dec 1862 (WELLES-D-1862-12-20).
- nerve above [contested]: Riddle lists him among those who led rejection of the Crittenden compromise in the 36th Congress (RIDDLE-WADE-p241) [single source, retrospective]; but on 20 Dec 1862 he and Collamer declined to say whether Seward should go (WELLES-D-1862-12-20; NH-6-p266-267). 
- experience high: Representative 1841-43; state house several terms; Senator from Feb 1854 (BDAC-fessenden_william_p; OCR date corrected from the vacancy-term text).
- reputation_1861 above: Member of the Washington Peace Convention of 1861 (BDAC-fessenden_william_p); chairman of Finance as war began (CONGDIR37-2-p22).
- Julian classes him with the Republican "conservatives" (JULIAN-PR-p356).

#### trumbull_lyman: Lyman Trumbull
- competence above: Chairman of Judiciary (CONGDIR37-2-p22). On 22 July 1861 he explained the Judiciary Committee's amendment freeing slaves used in the rebellion (NH-4-p380-381), the basis of the first Confiscation Act. Julian: he "greatly honored his position as chairman of the Judiciary Committee" and drafted the 13th Amendment (JULIAN-PR-p356, JULIAN-PR-p250) [own memoir; 13th Amendment introduced Jan 1864, outside the window].
- nerve above: Voted against the Crittenden-Johnson resolution in July 1861, with Breckinridge, Powell and Polk (RIDDLE-WADE-p242) [single source: Riddle says "on verbal grounds"; Nicolay and Hay say five dissenters, one on mere phraseology, NH-4-p379-380]. Called on Lincoln on 26 and 28 Oct 1861 with Wade and Chandler (NH-4-p467) and was named by McClellan as helping to press for Scott's retirement (MCCLELLAN-OS-p171) [rival memoir: McClellan]. Called "pointed, emphatic, and unequivocal" against Seward on 20 Dec 1862 and one of four who still said Seward should go when Lincoln took a vote (WELLES-D-1862-12-20; NH-6-p266-267).
- experience above: Illinois legislator and secretary of state, state supreme court 1848-53, Senator from March 1855 (BDAC-trumbull_lyman; OCR prints 1865, corrected).
- reputation_1861 above: Senator since 1855 and chairman of Judiciary in 1861 (BDAC-trumbull_lyman; CONGDIR37-2-p22). Julian groups him with the conservatives of the Republican side (JULIAN-PR-p356).
- antislavery: Confiscation amendment on slaves used for the rebellion (NH-4-p380-381); author of the 13th Amendment (JULIAN-PR-p250).

#### wilson_henry: Henry Wilson
- competence high: Chairman of Military Affairs. In the July-August 1861 session he introduced bills for volunteers, arms, aides, engineers, soldiers' pay and legalising the President's acts; Scott wrote on 10 Aug 1861 thanking him (NASON-WILSON-p306-308) [Nason is an admiring biography; Scott letter and the "twenty years" remark appear only there]. Julian: "great labors and invaluable services as chairman of the Military Committee" (JULIAN-PR-p357) [own memoir by a colleague].
- nerve high: Left the Whigs in 1848 when they rejected antislavery resolutions and ran as a Free-Soiler in 1852-53 (BDAC-wilson_henry). Was at Bull Run on 21 July 1861 (NASON-WILSON-p306-308). Raised a regiment as a sitting Senator (NASON-WILSON-p309).
- experience above: Massachusetts legislator 1841-42 and senate (president 1851-52); Senator from Jan 1855 (BDAC-wilson_henry).
- reputation_1861 above: Senator by coalition in 1855 and re-elected 1859 (BDAC-wilson_henry); chairman of Military Affairs at the start of the war (CONGDIR37-2-p22). Nason says Lincoln wanted to make him brigadier (NASON-WILSON-p309) [single source].
- antislavery: Withdrew from the Whig party in 1848 over the rejection of antislavery resolutions (BDAC-wilson_henry); "unswerving loyalty to freedom" (JULIAN-PR-p357).
- uses_press: Owned and edited the Boston Republican 1848-51 (BDAC-wilson_henry); in 1862 wrote an open letter denying that antislavery senators had interfered with McClellan's plans (PIERCE-SUMNER-p98-99, footnote) [his letter seen only through Pierce].
- organiser: Raised about 2,300 men in forty days for the 22nd and 23rd Massachusetts, a sharpshooter company and two batteries (NASON-WILSON-p309) [single source].
- Date conflict: Heitman gives colonel 21 Sept 1861 and resigned 29 Oct 1861 (HEIT1-wilson_henry); Nason gives commission 2 Sept and resignation 28 Oct (NASON-WILSON-p309, NASON-WILSON-p310).

#### blair_francis_p: Francis P. Blair Jr.
- competence above: Kept Missouri Unionists together in the Feb 1861 convention election: "to the courage, moderation, and tact of Francis P. Blair this result was greatly due" (BL-1-p263-267, p. 263) [rival memoir: Thomas L. Snead, Confederate staff officer]. With Lyon he planned the 10 May 1861 capture of Camp Jackson and had Harney relieved (BL-1-p263-267, pp. 265-266 [Snead]); McDowell writes that Missouri was "rescued from secession through the energy of General F. P. Blair" (BL-1-p171) [own-side memoir: McDowell]; Frémont: Lyon "together with Francis P. Blair, the younger, had saved Missouri from secession" (BL-1-p282) [own memoir: Frémont]. Lincoln wrote to him on 18 May 1861 about Harney (LINCOLN-1861-05-18-blair; read in NH-4-ch7). Listed first on the House Military Affairs Committee (CONGDIR37-2-p24-26).
- nerve high: As a slaveholder he advocated gradual emancipation in his 1856 campaign (NH-4-p207-208); had Harney, who made a truce with Price, relieved (BL-1-p263-267, p. 266 [Snead]); a leader in the Camp Jackson capture (BL-1-p263-267, p. 265 [Snead]).
- experience below: House 1857-59 plus a few weeks in June 1860; state house 1852-56 (BDAC-blair_francis_p).
- reputation_1861 above: Nicolay and Hay call him "a young, talented, courageous leader and skillful politician" whose followers cast 17,028 votes for Lincoln in Missouri (NH-4-p207-208); his brother was Lincoln's Postmaster General.
- slaveholder: "though himself a slaveholder" (NH-4-p207-208) [single source; no census read].
- antislavery: gradual emancipation advocate in 1856 and 1858 (NH-4-p207-208).
- Commission dates: Heitman (HEIT1-blair_francis_p). Controversy with Stevens in Jan 1864 is outside the window (WOODBURN-STEVENS-p226-227). Whether he exercised the colonelcy while sitting in Congress is needs-source.

#### washburne_elihu_b: Elihu B. Washburne
- competence average [contested]: Lincoln wrote to him on 13 Dec 1860 asking him to stop colleagues from entertaining compromise (LINCOLN-1860-12-13-washburne, NH-3-ch16), and on 21 Dec 1860 answered his report of an interview with General Scott (LINCOLN-1860-12-21-washburne; NH-3-p250); he met Lincoln at the station on 23 Feb 1861 with Seward (NH-3-ch20). Nicolay and Hay say Illinois officials gave Grant the 21st Illinois after Washburne "had warmly recommended" him (NH-4-p288). Against: Welles, a hostile diarist, says in Feb 1863 he had the reputation of being "the meanest man in the House" (WELLES-D-1863-02-07) [single source, hostile]. Trust and sponsorship, not proven skill at moving bills.
- nerve above [single source]: Riddle lists "E. B. Washburn" among House leaders who had "the courage" to reject the Crittenden compromise (RIDDLE-WADE-p241) [retrospective, partisan]; Lincoln relied on him to keep Illinois members "firm as a chain of steel" (LINCOLN-1860-12-13-washburne).
- experience above: Representative from 4 March 1853, eight years (BDAC-washburne_elihu_b).
- reputation_1861 unknown: no contemporary public judgement of him in March 1861 found; Lincoln's private trust is not public standing.
- Sponsor of Grant: Grant (own memoir) first met him at the Galena meeting of April 1861 (GRANT-PM-ch17) and says the Illinois delegation put his name first for brigadier (GRANT-PM-ch19) without naming Washburne; the sponsorship therefore rests on Nicolay and Hay (NH-4-p288) for the colonelcy and needs-source for the star (Washburne's letters or the Globe).
- Chair of House Commerce is inferred from first-listed (CONGDIR37-2-p24-26).

#### crittenden_john_j: John J. Crittenden
- competence above: Nicolay and Hay call him "the real leader of the compromise movement" in Dec 1860-Jan 1861, "a man of national rank and authority" (NH-3-p223-224); the compromise failed in the Senate (25 to 23 for the Clark substitute) (NH-3-p227). On 22 July 1861 his war-aims resolution passed the House with almost no opposition and his Senate counterpart passed too (NH-4-p379-380; RIDDLE-WADE-p242: 117 to 2 in the House).
- nerve above [single source]: Pressed the compromise against the Republican majority and for the Union against secession ("unconditional devotion to the Union", NH-3-p223-224); Nicolay and Hay cite his "devoted loyalty" in the 37th Congress (NH-4-p368). The record does not show a specific risk taken in 1861-62 beyond opposing confiscation.
- experience high: Senator 1817-19, 1835-41, 1842-48, 1855-61; Attorney General twice; Governor of Kentucky (BDAC-crittenden_john_j).
- reputation_1861 high: "A man of national rank and authority" (NH-3-p223-224); a correspondent of Lincoln's wrote in Dec 1860 that he was "one of the most patriotic and at the same time mischievous of the Southern Senators" (NH-3-p256) [contested]; Cox names him among the Senate's moderate "breakwater" (COX-3DEC-p75-76) [own memoir: Cox, a Democrat].
- opposes_emancipation: In the Aug 1861 confiscation debate he argued the measure violated the Constitution (NH-4-p382). Slaveholder: needs-source.
- Conflicts: Riddle gives his age as seventy-five (RIDDLE-WADE-p242), Nicolay and Hay as seventy-two in Jan 1861 (NH-3-p223-224); BDAC birth 10 Sept 1787. House vote on his resolution: Riddle 117 to 2 (RIDDLE-WADE-p242); Nicolay and Hay "four dissenting votes" (NH-4-p379-380).

#### vallandigham_clement_l: Clement L. Vallandigham
- competence average [contested]: On 5 Dec 1862 he offered resolutions against making the war one of subjugation or emancipation; they were laid on the table 79 to 50 (APPLETONS-vallandigham_clement_l; Appletons is a reference work, vote not checked against the Globe). Nicolay and Hay say his opposition and "dilatory parliamentary tactics" helped keep the Missouri emancipation bill from a vote (NH-6-p397).
- nerve high: Appletons: "bold utterances against the acts of the administration" and the 14 Jan 1863 speech calling the war a failed experiment (APPLETONS-vallandigham_clement_l); arrested in 1863 and banished through the lines (BDAC-vallandigham_clement_l; after the window). Cox, a Democratic colleague: "held his position immovably against a terrific current" (COX-3DEC-p80-82) [own-side memoir].
- experience below: Ohio house 1846-47; House from 25 May 1858, under three years by March 1861 (BDAC-vallandigham_clement_l; Cox confirms the date, COX-3DEC-p80-82).
- reputation_1861 above: Cox: the 1858 contest "fixed Vallandigham as one of the most conspicuous of our public men" (COX-3DEC-p80-82) [own-side memoir, single source]; Cox elsewhere lists him among those then "accounted moderate and middle men" (COX-3DEC-p75-76).
- opposes_emancipation: Dec 1862 resolutions (APPLETONS-vallandigham_clement_l); obstruction of the Missouri bill (NH-6-p397).
- uses_press: Edited the Western Empire in Dayton 1847-49 (BDAC-vallandigham_clement_l).
- Not read: his brother's 1872 Life (partisan).

#### pendleton_george_h: George H. Pendleton
- competence unknown: Searched BDAC, Cox, Nicolay and Hay 3-6, Welles; no judgement of his skill in 1861-62. He spoke against the Legal Tender bill with Spaulding, Conkling and Morrill (NH-6-p234-235), a debate he did not shape.
- nerve unknown: no documented political risk in 1861-62.
- experience average: State senate 1854-56; House from 1857 (BDAC-pendleton_george_h), four years by March 1861.
- reputation_1861 average [single source]: Cox lists Pendleton and Vallandigham among those "accounted moderate and middle men" in the 36th Congress (COX-3DEC-p75-76) [own-side memoir]; later nominated for Vice President in 1864 (BDAC-pendleton_george_h).

#### wood_benjamin: Benjamin Wood
- competence unknown: Searched BDAC, Appletons (no entry for him), Hudson 1873, Nicolay and Hay 3-6, Welles, Moore's Rebellion Record vol 2 (names only the paper). No judgement of his political skill found; needs-source (Globe, New York press).
- nerve unknown: the Daily News was presented by a grand jury with four other papers in Aug 1861 (REBREC-2-D67), but the entry does not name Wood.
- experience low: first elected in 1860; BDAC lists no earlier office (BDAC-wood_benjamin).
- reputation_1861 unknown: no judgement found.
- uses_press: Bought the Daily News in 1860 and edited and published it until death (BDAC-wood_benjamin); the paper was presented in Aug 1861 as aiding treason (REBREC-2-D67).

#### lane_james_h: James H. Lane
- competence above: On 18 April 1861 he organised the Frontier Guard and led it into the East Room (NH-4-p106-107); Lincoln authorised him to raise a Kansas brigade (NH-5-p83-84), and he was appointed brigadier on 18 Dec 1861 over Halleck's protest (HEIT1-lane_james_h; NH-5-p83-84). Lincoln wrote on 31 Jan 1862 that Lane had "assented" to serving under Hunter "as often as told" (LINCOLN-1862-01-31-war-secretary; NH-5-p84) and on 10 Feb that Lane must report to Hunter or decline (LINCOLN-1862-02-10-hunter; NH-5-p85). The appointment was canceled on 21 March 1862 (HEIT1-lane_james_h). Rated above, not high, because the canceled commission shows the limit.
- nerve high: Nicolay and Hay call Lane and Cassius Clay "both men of mark and courage" who raised companies in Washington on 18 April 1861 (NH-4-p106-107).
- experience above: Mexican War colonel, Lieutenant Governor of Indiana, Representative 1853-55, Topeka and Leavenworth conventions (BDAC-lane_james_h).
- reputation_1861 above: Elected to the Senate under the Topeka constitution in 1856 and again in 1861 (BDAC-lane_james_h); "men of mark" (NH-4-p106-107).
- ambitious: "Lane was, indeed, chargeable with a selfish ambition," and tried to supplant Hunter (NH-5-p84).
- harsh_with_civilians: Halleck wrote that Lane's men "rob, steal, plunder, and burn" across the Missouri line (NH-5-p83-84; Halleck's report OR series I vol 8 p 509 was not read) [charge, as quoted; Nicolay and Hay judge Halleck's tone "somewhat harsh" and partly prejudiced].
- antislavery: Free-State movement in Kansas 1855-61 (BDAC-lane_james_h; needs a direct source).

#### Proposed tag (not used in the CSV)

- presses_command: pressing the President or commanders on military moves or appointments from Congress (Wade, Chandler, Trumbull on 26 and 28 Oct 1861, NH-4-p467; the JCCW's visits Jan-Mar 1862, JULIAN-PR-p205; Lane and Hunter, NH-5-p84).

### Governors: Union, border and Confederate (`governors`)

Flags: [single source], [own memoir], [rival memoir: author], [contested]. Peer group for the stat scale: governors of 1861-62. Page keys for the OR are the printed page on which the passage starts.

#### magoffin_beriah: Beriah Magoffin
- competence below: neutrality failed; the legislature passed over his vetoes (resolution to demand Confederate evacuation, Gen. Anderson's enrolling a force, Crittenden's command) and he resigned in August 1862 (APPLETONS-magoffin_beriah) [single source].
- nerve above: answered the War Department on 15 Apr 1861 that Kentucky would "furnish no troops for the wicked purpose of subduing her sister Southern States" (OR-III-1-p70); on 19 Aug 1861 asked Lincoln to withdraw the troops in Kentucky and was refused on 24 Aug (NH-4-p241).
- experience average: police judge 1840, state senate 1850, repeated elector and delegate, governor since Sept 1859 (APPLETONS-magoffin_beriah).
- reputation_1861 average: elected governor in 1859; Feb 1861 message urged a border-state convention; proposed in 1860 amendments Northern Democrats might accept (APPLETONS-magoffin_beriah). No press comment found. [single source]
- Message to the legislature that Kentucky would resist Republican policy "to the death, if necessary": quoted by Nicolay and Hay (NH-4-p230), not a contemporary.
- Traits: none tagged. Slaveholding: needs-source.

#### hicks_thomas_h: Thomas H. Hicks
- competence above: on 17 Apr 1861 obtained from the Secretary of War written confirmation that Maryland's four regiments were for defence of Washington and Maryland only (OR-III-1-p79); on 13 May 1861 reported he would call the regiments as volunteers (OR-III-1-p190); Appletons says he refused to call a special session on secession despite the senate majority (APPLETONS-hicks_thomas_h).
- nerve average: asked for terms before acting; wrote on 28 Aug 1861 that he had "heretofore been indisposed to see arms disbursed here" (OR-III-1-p463). Issued a proclamation after the Baltimore riot (APPLETONS-hicks_thomas_h).
- experience above: sheriff 1824, legislature 1836, governor's council 1837, register of wills 1838, convention 1849, governor 1858 (BDAC-hicks_thomas_h; APPLETONS-hicks_thomas_h).
- reputation_1861 above (first appearance, Aug 1861): Kent County Unionists wrote "many, many thanks to you" for saving the State from revolution (OR-III-1-p463, letter of S. W. Spencer, 24 Aug 1861) [single source].
- Declined brigadier general July 1862 (BDAC OCR). [contested] Death 13 Feb (Appletons) vs 14 Feb 1865 (BDAC).

#### gamble_hamilton_r: Hamilton R. Gamble
- competence above: authorised 5 Aug 1861 to raise fifteen regiments (OR-III-1-p388); on 7 Nov 1861 named an agent to carry out his arrangement with the President for arming and paying Missouri State militia (OR-III-1-p623).
- nerve above: accepted office from the State convention on 31 Jul 1861 and "announced his unconditional adherence to the Union" amid guerrilla war (NH-4-p225) [single source].
- experience unknown: no register read covers his career before 1861. Searched Appletons (entry head lost in OCR), BDAC (no entry).
- reputation_1861 above (first appearance, July 1861): chosen governor by the convention, described as a conservative (NH-4-p225). Co-signed the 28 Jun 1862 governors' letter to Lincoln (OR-III-2-p180).
- Slaveholding: needs-source.

#### jackson_claiborne_f: Claiborne F. Jackson
- competence below: his plan to take Missouri out failed; fled Jefferson City 15 Jun 1861 and Boonville 17 Jun (NH-4-p224); State convention declared offices vacant and installed Gamble (NH-4-p225).
- nerve above: replied on 17 Apr 1861 that the requisition was "illegal, unconstitutional, and revolutionary" and "not one man" would be furnished (OR-III-1-p82); asked Davis for arms and told the Confederate Secretary of War Missouri was "yet under the tyranny of Lincoln's Government" (NH-4-p211).
- experience above: twelve years in the legislature, speaker, bank commissioner (APPLETONS-jackson_claiborne_f) [single source].
- reputation_1861 average: elected governor 1860 (APPLETONS-jackson_claiborne_f).
- Appletons says "deposed by the legislature" and calls him a Confederate brigadier general; neither agrees with NH-4-p225 / no register: [contested], not used.
- Traits: none tagged. Slaveholding: needs-source.

#### pierpont_francis_h: Francis H. Pierpont
- competence unknown, nerve unknown, experience unknown, reputation_1861 unknown. Searched OR III-1 and III-2, Nicolay and Hay vols 3-6, Chase diary, Appletons OCR (no entry), BDAC (not a member).
- candid: told Cameron on 19 Aug 1861 the home guards numbered no more than 1,500 and could not be relied on for service (OR-III-1-p431); on 19 May 1862 told Adjutant General Thomas he feared he could not raise a regiment in reasonable time (OR-III-2-p46).
- Authorised 3 Aug 1861 to raise eight infantry and two cavalry regiments (OR-III-1-p387); present among the governors in Washington 26 Sep 1862 (CHASE-D-1862-09-26).

#### curtin_andrew_g: Andrew G. Curtin
- competence above: Pennsylvania furnished 270 regiments in his term (BDAC-curtin_andrew_g); on 6 Sep 1862 proposed the meeting of loyal governors (PEARSON-ANDREW-v2-p48); co-signed the 28 Jun 1862 letter to Lincoln (OR-III-2-p180).
- nerve above: inaugural address advocated forcible suppression; kept Gen. Patterson's 25,000 under arms at state cost when the War Department revoked the call (APPLETONS-curtin_andrew_g) [single source]. Welles later called him "excitable and easily alarmed" (WELLES-D-1863-06-15; June 1863, about military panic, not used).
- experience average: Whig campaigner, elector 1848, secretary of the commonwealth 1855-58 (APPLETONS-curtin_andrew_g); no elective office before 1861.
- reputation_1861 above: elected in 1860 by about 32,000 (APPLETONS-curtin_andrew_g).
- organiser: Reserve and 270 regiments (BDAC-curtin_andrew_g; APPLETONS-curtin_andrew_g). [contested] Birth year 1815 (Appletons) vs 1817 (BDAC).

#### andrew_john_a: John A. Andrew
- competence high: by 15 Apr 1861 had companies ordered into Boston and urged doubling output at Springfield Armory (OR-III-1-p71); on 17 Apr 1861 proposed raising Gordon's regiment and reported troops moving (OR-III-1-p78); sent a confidential call to the governors of Maine and New Hampshire before the war (APPLETONS-andrew_john_a).
- nerve high: pressed emancipation and Black troops in 1862 and drafted the Altoona address (APPLETONS-andrew_john_a); wrote on 6 Sep 1862 that he was trying to "save the Prest. from the infamy of ruining his country" (PEARSON-ANDREW-v2-p48).
- experience below: legislature 1858-60 only (APPLETONS-andrew_john_a).
- reputation_1861 above: largest popular vote cast to that date for any candidate; radicalism feared by managers (APPLETONS-andrew_john_a) [single source]. In Washington two days after Altoona; Chase left a card for him on 26 Sep 1862 (CHASE-D-1862-09-26).
- antislavery: APPLETONS-andrew_john_a; PEARSON-ANDREW-v2-p48. disdains_superiors: PEARSON-ANDREW-v2-p48 (own letter, one instance).
- BDAC's John F. Andrew is his son.

#### morton_oliver_h_p_t: Oliver P. Morton
- competence high: tendered 10,000 men on 15 Apr 1861 (OR-III-1-p70); 16 Apr: six regiments to be full in three days (OR-III-1-p75); obtained $2,000,000 borrowing authority (APPLETONS-morton_oliver_h_p_t); ran the state arsenal at a profit of $75,000 (FOULKE-MORTON-v1; Foulke is a 1899 biography by his associate; OCR has no page numbers) [single source].
- nerve high: after the Democratic legislature of 1862 failed to organise he financed the state through a bureau of finance from April 1863 until January 1865; the supreme court condemned the course (APPLETONS-morton_oliver_h_p_t).
- experience average: circuit judge 1852, candidate for governor 1856, lieutenant governor 1860 (BDAC-morton_oliver_h_p_t; APPLETONS-morton_oliver_h_p_t).
- reputation_1861 average: unsuccessful in 1856; elected lieutenant governor 1860.
- Signed the Altoona address (FOULKE-MORTON-v1) and the 28 Jun 1862 letter (OR-III-2-p180 not tagged: his name is in the printed list).

#### yates_richard: Richard Yates
- competence average: 17 Apr 1861 forty companies tendered, six regiments soon full (OR-III-1-p80); asked Washington for arms and for control of the St Louis arsenal's stores (OR-III-1-p80). No finding either way on administration. [single source for each]
- nerve above: took military possession of Cairo and called the legislature on 12 Apr 1861 (APPLETONS-yates_richard) [single source].
- experience above: legislature 1842-49, Congress 1851-55 (BDAC-yates_richard).
- reputation_1861 average: elected governor 1860.
- antislavery: "outspoken opponent of slavery" (APPLETONS-yates_richard). Commissioned Grant colonel of the 21st Illinois after the regiment refused its elected colonel (GRANT-PM-ch18) [own memoir: Grant].

#### morgan_edwin_d: Edwin D. Morgan
- competence above: asked on 15 Apr 1861 for the object and numbers of the call before the legislature adjourned (OR-III-1-p72) and on 17 Apr for terms (OR-III-1-p83); report of 26 Nov 1862 shows 82,739 three-year volunteers from New York since 2 Jul 1862 (OR-III-2-p881).
- nerve average: no evidence of risk-taking beyond duty.
- experience above: city alderman 1849, state senate 1850-55, RNC chairman 1856-64 (BDAC-morgan_edwin_d); maj gen vols 28 Sep 1861 (HEIT1-morgan_edwin_d).
- reputation_1861 above: chairman of the Republican National Committee, delegate 1856 and 1860 (BDAC-morgan_edwin_d).

#### dennison_william: William Dennison
- competence above: asked Lincoln for the largest share of the 75,000 and was given thirteen regiments (OR-III-1-p73); commissioned McClellan major general of Ohio troops in April 1861 (NH-4-p285, Nicolay and Hay, 1890).
- nerve above: wrote 22 Apr 1861 that with no word from Washington he was "compelled to assume extraordinary responsibilities" (OR-III-1-p102).
- experience unknown: public-domain career before 1860 not read. reputation_1861 unknown.
- organiser: two regiments moving within a week of the call (OR-III-1-p77).

#### tod_david: David Tod
- competence above: Welles in January 1863 called his message "manly, wholesome, and vigorous" (WELLES-D-1863-01-10) [single source; judgement about a message, rival-party cabinet colleague].
- nerve, experience, reputation_1861: unknown. Searched OR III-1, III-2 and Welles; Appletons has no entry in the local OCR.
- First appearance as governor: OR-III-1-p922 (7 Mar 1862).

#### seymour_horatio: Horatio Seymour
- competence high: elected governor 1852; Speaker 1845; elected governor again 1862 over Wadsworth by 10,752 (APPLETONS-seymour_horatio); Welles allows him "smartness" (WELLES-D-1862-09-27 [rival memoir: Welles]).
- nerve above: nominated 1862 on a platform of loyalty and the right to criticise the administration (APPLETONS-seymour_horatio).
- experience high: assembly, mayor of Utica, speaker, governor 1853-54 (APPLETONS-seymour_horatio).
- reputation_1861 above: declined Buchanan's mission 1857; spoke at the Albany compromise convention Jan 1861 (APPLETONS-seymour_horatio).
- ambitious: Welles, "inveterate partisan, place-hunter" (WELLES-D-1862-09-27). opposes_emancipation: inaugural message, 7 Jan 1863 (APPLETONS-seymour_horatio).
- [contested] bloc: his 28 Oct 1861 speech and recruiting work (APPLETONS-seymour_horatio) vs Welles's judgement; see CSV.

#### washburn_israel: Israel Washburn Jr.
- competence average: answered the call on 15 Apr 1861 promising prompt response from all parties (OR-III-1-p71); no further evidence.
- nerve unknown. Searched OR III-1 and III-2.
- experience above: Maine house 1842-43, Congress 1851-61 (BDAC-washburn_israel; APPLETONS-washburn_israel).
- reputation_1861 average: elected governor on resigning from Congress.
- [contested] death 12 May (BDAC OCR) vs 13 May 1883 (Appletons).

#### ramsey_alexander: Alexander Ramsey
- competence unknown, nerve unknown. Dakota War (1862), recorded neutrally: on 25-26 Aug 1862 he asked the War Department for a month's extension of the draft and for muster by companies because of the "Indian panic"; Stanton refused the month's extension but authorised him to fix the draft date on his own responsibility (OR-III-2-p455, p465, p467); a Lincoln telegram of 27 Aug on the same point is only noted in the OR (content not read). Searched OR III-2; OR I vol 13 and Minnesota state documents not read: needs-source for his orders and statements.
- experience high: Congress 1843-47, territorial governor 1849-53, mayor of St Paul 1855 (BDAC-ramsey_alexander; APPLETONS-ramsey_alexander).
- reputation_1861 average: lost the 1857 race, governor from 1860 (BDAC-ramsey_alexander).

#### letcher_john: John Letcher
- competence average: told Richmond in a letter that the War Secretary's enlisting of companies out of Virginia regiments would break them up, and that the State was left in ignorance (OR-IV-1-p969, date needs-source).
- nerve above: refused the 15 Apr 1861 requisition on his own authority (OR-III-1-p76); placed state forces at the Confederacy's disposal before the popular vote (APPLETONS-letcher_john).
- experience high: convention 1850, Congress 1851-59 (BDAC-letcher_john).
- reputation_1861 average: elected governor 1859; BDAC credits him with the peace convention of Feb 1861.
- Slaveholding: needs-source. [contested] birth and death days (BDAC vs Appletons).

#### brown_joseph_e: Joseph E. Brown
- competence above: telegraphed Davis 7 Aug 1861 about camps of instruction and 51,000 men awaiting orders (OR-IV-1-p535); Appletons: put 10,000 state men in the field in 1864 and refused to send them out of State (APPLETONS-brown_joseph_e).
- nerve high: seized Forts Pulaski and Jackson on 8 Jan 1861 and the Augusta arsenal before secession (APPLETONS-brown_joseph_e) [single source].
- experience above: senate 1849, judge 1855, governor from 1857 (BDAC-brown_joseph_e).
- reputation_1861 above: re-elected in 1859 and 1861 (BDAC-brown_joseph_e).
- quarrelsome: complaints to Davis and Walker (OR-IV-1-p535); disputed conscription with Davis (APPLETONS-brown_joseph_e). makes_policy: seizures above. Slaveholding: needs-source.

#### harris_isham_g: Isham G. Harris
- competence above: Appletons says he "exhibited ability and resource" until driven from the state (APPLETONS-harris_isham_g) [single source].
- nerve above: 17 Apr 1861 refused a man for "coercion" but offered 50,000 for defence (OR-III-1-p81).
- experience high: senate 1847, Congress 1849-53, governor from 1857 (BDAC-harris_isham_g).
- reputation_1861 above: elected 1857, 1859, 1861 (BDAC-harris_isham_g).
- Appletons: his father owned ten or twelve enslaved people; own holdings needs-source.

#### pickens_francis_w: Francis W. Pickens
- competence average: set terms on 28 Dec 1860 to avoid "irregular collisions" at Fort Sumter (OR-I-1-p113); later letters on officers and regiments (OR-IV-1, not tagged). No finding either way.
- nerve above: 29 Dec 1860 ordered Col. Cunningham to take the Charleston arsenal (OR-I-1-p7); through Hayne demanded surrender of Sumter (OR-I-1-p166).
- experience high: Congress 1834-43, minister to Russia 1858-60 (BDAC-pickens_francis_w).
- reputation_1861 above: minister to Russia. Slaveholding: needs-source (BDAC says planter).

#### vance_zebulon_b: Zebulon B. Vance
- competence above: sent agents abroad and bought a blockade-runner that supplied state troops (APPLETONS-vance_zebulon_b) [single source; beyond 1862].
- nerve above: urged Davis in Dec 1863 to seek negotiation (APPLETONS-vance_zebulon_b) [single source].
- experience average: House 1858-61, state house 1854 (BDAC-vance_zebulon_b); captain May 1861, colonel Aug 1861.
- reputation_1861 average: young Congressman who opposed secession then raised a company.
- organiser: APPLETONS-vance_zebulon_b. Slaveholding: needs-source.

### Diplomats and editors (`diplomats_editors`)

Dates of first appearance and page forms: Weed Memoir pages are the printed pages of vol. 2 (1884) read from OCR; Richardson items are cited by dispatch date (the OCR has no reliable page numbers); Newton (Gutenberg text) is cited by chapter.

#### adams_charles_f: Charles Francis Adams
- competence high: Seward's dispatch of Dec 1861 says Adams's conduct commanded esteem and respect (PRFA-1862-GB-index, read from the volume's list of papers; body page not verified) [single source]. Told Russell on 17 May 1861 that the US might end his mission unless Russell's language to his predecessor was clarified (NEWTON-LYONS-ch3).
- nerve above: that 17 May 1861 interview put the break-off on the table to Russell (NEWTON-LYONS-ch3) [single source].
- experience above: Mass. House 1831, Senate 1835-40, Representative 1859-61; no prior diplomatic post (BDAC-adams_charles_f).
- reputation_1861 high: son and grandson of presidents; Free-Soil vice-presidential nominee 1848; elected to Congress 1858 (BDAC-adams_charles_f, APPLETONS-adams_charles_f).
- candid: told Weed in London on 6 Dec 1861 that war was seriously contemplated and he saw no way to avert it except releasing Mason and Slidell (WEED-MEM-p350) [own memoir: Weed].
- Welles records Sumner calling Adams too reserved and retiring (WELLES-D1-p301, 1863) [single source; outside 1861-62].

#### dayton_william_l: William L. Dayton
- competence average: wrote a reasoned lawyer's case that occasional blockade-running did not destroy a blockade (dispatch of 12 Feb 1862, PRFA-1862-p313); worked with Bigelow and Sanford rather than alone, and called Weed to Paris in Jan 1862 (WEED-MEM-p390, WEED-MEM-p349) [own memoir: Weed; single source for judgement].
- nerve unknown: no documented act of risk-taking found; searched Weed Memoir and the 1861-62 dispatch list.
- experience high: judge 1838-41, US Senator 1842-61, NJ attorney general 1857-61 (BDAC-dayton_william_l).
- reputation_1861 above: Republican vice-presidential nominee 1856 (BDAC-dayton_william_l).
- Weed thanked Adams and Dayton for accepting unofficial help (WEED-MEM-p414) [own memoir].

#### clay_cassius_m: Cassius M. Clay
- competence unknown: PRFA-1861-p286-288 shows a cordial reception in St Petersburg in June 1861 (Gortchakoff, audience 28 June) but nothing that measures his skill; Confederate commissioners judged his letters and speeches harmful to the Union cause (RICH-II-1861-06-01-yancey) [adversary view]. Searched PRFA-1861 Russia section, Welles.
- nerve high: armed his Lexington newspaper office against a mob in 1845; captured in the Mexican War, Jan 1847 (APPLETONS-clay_cassius_m) [partly from his own memoir as quoted by Appletons].
- experience average: Kentucky legislator 1835-37, 1840; Mexican War captain (HEIT1-clay_cassius_m, APPLETONS-clay_cassius_m); no national or diplomatic office before 1861.
- reputation_1861 above: nationally known antislavery editor; candidate for governor 1850; campaigned for Lincoln 1860 (APPLETONS-clay_cassius_m).
- antislavery: converted to abolition at Yale, edited True American (APPLETONS-clay_cassius_m).
- quarrelsome: Appletons says he was continually in quarrels and bloody personal encounters (APPLETONS-clay_cassius_m) [single source].
- Heitman: capt 1 Ky cav 9 June 1846; maj gen vols 11 Apr 1862; resigned 11 Mar 1863 (HEIT1-clay_cassius_m). Welles, 8 Sept 1862: told that Clay advocated an armistice; Lincoln doubted he was rightly understood (WELLES-D-1862-09-08).

#### mason_james_m: James M. Mason
- competence average: wrote persistent, correct dispatches; his 7 July 1862 letter to Russell got only a formal acknowledgement from Layard (RICH-II-1862-07-30-mason) [single source].
- nerve above: ran the blockade from Charleston in Oct 1861 and was seized from the Trent and held at Fort Warren (RICH-II-1861-10-05-mason, BDAC-mason_james_m, NEWTON-LYONS-ch3).
- experience high: Representative 1837-39, Senator 1847-61 (BDAC-mason_james_m).
- reputation_1861 above: sitting Senator for 14 years (BDAC-mason_james_m).
- uses_press: had extracts of Richmond dispatches printed in London as from a trusted Southern source (RICH-II-1862-07-30-mason).
- slaveholder: needs-source.

#### slidell_john: John Slidell
- competence above: on 26 Mar 1862 told Richmond that the Emperor would follow England's lead, a correct reading (RICH-II-1862-03-26-slidell); on 9 Oct 1862 tracked Thouvenel and Persigny on recognition after Antietam (RICH-II-1862-10-09-slidell). Proposed a commercial convention to France that was not accepted (APPLETONS-slidell_john) [single source for competence].
- nerve above: seized from the Trent, imprisoned, and resumed the mission (NEWTON-LYONS-ch3, BDAC-slidell_john).
- experience high: Representative, Senator, minister-to-Mexico mission 1845 (BDAC-slidell_john).
- reputation_1861 above: Senator and confidential friend of Buchanan; declined a cabinet post (APPLETONS-slidell_john).
- candid: reported to Richmond that France would not move without England (RICH-II-1862-03-26-slidell). He also wrote that he never argued slavery with European friends (RICH-II-1862-02-11-slidell).

#### yancey_william_l: William L. Yancey
- competence average: told Toombs on 1 June 1861 that the British cabinet had no settled policy and would delay recognition until Confederate advantage or cotton need (RICH-II-1861-06-01-yancey); cultivated MP Lindsay to press ministers (RICH-II-1861-11-08-yancey). No recognition obtained.
- nerve high: leader of the extreme party; 1858 letter urging committees of safety to "fire the southern heart"; reported the secession ordinance (APPLETONS-yancey_william_l).
- experience above: Representative 1844-46, state legislator, editor (BDAC-yancey_william_l).
- reputation_1861 high: chosen chairman of the commission (BDAC-yancey_william_l, RICH-II-1861-03-16-toombs).
- Complained on 27 Jan 1862 that the State Department sent only news of Leesburg; resigned 22 Mar 1862 (RICH-II-1862-01-27-yancey, RICH-II-1862-03-22-yancey).

#### lyons_richard_b_p: Lord Lyons
- competence high: delivered Russell's Trent demand with such tact that Seward acknowledged the help (DNB-lyons_richard_b_p).
- nerve below: declined a peremptory demand before orders; "habitual caution" on Mercier's plan; his Trent silence called "almost superhuman" reticence (DNB-lyons_richard_b_p, NEWTON-LYONS-ch3, NEWTON-LYONS-ch4) [biographer, 1913].
- experience high: attache 1839, envoy to Florence 1858, minister at Washington Dec 1858 (DNB-lyons_richard_b_p).
- reputation_1861 above: K.C.B. 1860 (DNB-lyons_richard_b_p).
- candid: wrote in Nov 1861 he was not confident of keeping relations unchanged (NEWTON-LYONS-ch3).

#### mercier_henri: Henri Mercier
- competence average: bent on visiting Richmond in April 1862; Lyons predicted trouble (NEWTON-LYONS-ch4) [single source; partisan].
- nerve above: went to Richmond against US wishes; pushed French-led mediation in Oct 1862 (NEWTON-LYONS-ch4).
- experience unknown, reputation_1861 unknown: needs-source. Searched DNB, Newton, Weed, Welles, Papers volumes; no biography found.

#### russell_john: Earl Russell
- competence above: held the Trent line, "a plain Yes or No" in seven days (NEWTON-LYONS-ch3); judged the blockade legally effective and wished to offer mediation in Sept 1862 but failed to stop the Alabama (DNB-russell_john).
- nerve above: pressed mediation in Sept 1862, ready to risk war over the Trent (DNB-russell_john, NEWTON-LYONS-ch3).
- experience high: Prime Minister 1846-52; Foreign Secretary 1852-53 and from 1859 (DNB-russell_john).
- reputation_1861 high: former Prime Minister; peerage July 1861 (DNB-russell_john).

#### greeley_horace: Horace Greeley
- competence above: ran the largest radical paper; Recollections claims he urged an occupation of Richmond by early June 1861 (GREELEY-RBL-p402-403) [own memoir].
- nerve above: Dana says Greeley wanted him out in April 1862 because Greeley was for peace (DANA-RCW-ch1) [rival memoir: Dana] [contested]; in Hay's July 1864 diary "G" at Niagara urged talks with Southern agents (HAY-D-1864-07-20) [single source; "G" read as Greeley].
- experience high: founded the Tribune 1841; Representative 1848-49 (BDAC-greeley_horace).
- reputation_1861 high: Republican convention delegate 1860; Senate candidate 1861 (BDAC-greeley_horace).
- uses_press: he wrote the paper's war cries himself (GREELEY-RBL-p402-403) [own memoir].
- antislavery: Tribune was a leading organ of antislavery sentiment (APPLETONS-dana_charles_a).
- ambitious: unsuccessful Senate candidate 1861; Dana worked for his nomination (BDAC-greeley_horace, APPLETONS-dana_charles_a).

#### bennett_james_g: James Gordon Bennett
- competence above: news-gathering excellence; 63 war correspondents; circulation more than doubled (APPLETONS-bennett_james_g) [single source].
- nerve unknown: no documented act found.
- experience high: sole proprietor of the Herald since 1835 (APPLETONS-bennett_james_g).
- reputation_1861 unknown: Appletons gives only the notoriety of the 1830s.
- uses_press: dictated editorials to his staff (APPLETONS-bennett_james_g). Welles, 10 Aug 1862: Weed-Bennett reconciliation, then the Herald changed its tune (WELLES-D-1862-08-10) [rival diary: Welles].

#### raymond_henry_j: Henry J. Raymond
- competence above: founded the Times 1851; backed Lincoln though deploring a hesitating policy; proposed a provisional government after Bull Run (APPLETONS-raymond_henry_j) [single source]. Welles calls him "pliant" to Weed (WELLES-D1-p184) [rival diary: Welles].
- nerve unknown: none documented.
- experience high: Speaker 1851, Lieutenant Governor 1854 (BDAC-raymond_henry_j).
- reputation_1861 above: delegate to the 1860 Republican convention; Speaker again 1862 (BDAC-raymond_henry_j).
- uses_press: edited the Times (APPLETONS-raymond_henry_j).

#### weed_thurlow: Thurlow Weed
- competence high: his Europe mission turned opinion, per his own account and Appletons (WEED-MEM-p349, WEED-MEM-p414, APPLETONS-weed_thurlow) [own memoir].
- nerve above: sailed the day after the Trent seizure into a war scare and published in the London Times (WEED-MEM-p349) [own memoir].
- experience high: Journal editor since 1830, thirty-five years in control (APPLETONS-weed_thurlow).
- reputation_1861 high: party manager; Welles calls him Seward's factotum (WELLES-D-1862-08-10) [rival diary: Welles].
- intrigues: Welles says Seward and Weed used corruptionists (WELLES-D-1862-08-10); Appletons says Evarts's backers went to Harris at Weed's instigation in the 1861 Senate caucus (APPLETONS-dana_charles_a) [single source].
- uses_press: Welles says the Times followed Weed; Weed reconciled with Bennett and the Herald changed (WELLES-D-1862-08-10, WEED-MEM-p349).
- Returned June 1862 (WEED-MEM-p413); retired from the Journal Jan 1863 after his advice was rejected (WEED-MEM-p430) [own memoir].

#### dana_charles_a: Charles A. Dana
- competence above: managing editor fifteen years; trusted by Stanton (APPLETONS-dana_charles_a, DANA-RCW-ch1) [own memoir].
- nerve above: says he favoured war while Greeley favoured peace and left (DANA-RCW-ch1) [own memoir].
- experience above: Tribune from 1847, part owner (APPLETONS-dana_charles_a).
- reputation_1861 above: credited with the Tribune's influence (APPLETONS-dana_charles_a) [single source].
- antislavery: Tribune under Greeley and Dana was a leading antislavery organ (APPLETONS-dana_charles_a).
- Death date, commissioner start date: needs-source.

#### marble_manton_m: Manton Marble
- competence, nerve, reputation_1861 unknown: only career facts found (APPLETONS-marble_manton_m). Searched Welles, Newton, Weed.
- experience below: journalist since 1855, 26 years old in 1862 (APPLETONS-marble_manton_m).
- Bloc: needs-source.

#### daniel_john_m: John M. Daniel
- competence above: Examiner called the leading paper of the South (APPLETONS-daniel_john_m) [single source].
- nerve high: fought several duels, one in 1864 with a shattered arm (APPLETONS-daniel_john_m).
- experience above: Examiner staff from 1847, minister to Sardinia (APPLETONS-daniel_john_m).
- reputation_1861 above: leading paper; early secession advocate (APPLETONS-daniel_john_m).
- quarrelsome: duels (APPLETONS-daniel_john_m). disdains_superiors: attacked Jefferson Davis with severity (APPLETONS-daniel_john_m).
- Appletons says his pro-slavery radicalism drew on a Carlyle reading treating Black people as not men in the white sense; recorded neutrally for the sensitive-history gate.

### Confederate executive, bureaus and Congress (`confederate_executive`)

Reading notes. Jones (A Rebel War Clerk's Diary, 1866) is a clerk's view with strong prejudices, including anti-Jewish remarks and distrust of Northern-born officers. Chesnut (1905 edition) was condensed and revised for publication (Introduction, p.xxi-xxii) and gives day-month only. Rhett Jr. (Battles and Leaders vol 1, "The Confederate Government at Montgomery") is a hostile witness to Davis and his cabinet. The Official Records copy read (OR IV vol 1) is poor OCR; pages are taken from the nearest page-number line. OR II vol 5 and OR IV vol 2 could not be read.

#### davis_jefferson: Jefferson Davis
- competence average [contested]: Barnwell, a supporter, told Rhett Davis was "not a man of great ability" but just and honourable (BL-1-p103); Rhett called him egotistical and arrogant (BL-1-p102) [rival memoir: Rhett Jr.]. Jones wrote in June 1861 that no Executive had had such "cordial and unanimous" support (JONES-I-1861-06-17) and in Oct 1862 relayed that many call him a "small specimen of a statesman" (JONES-I-1862-10-31) [clerk's view].
- nerve high: wounded at Buena Vista as colonel of the Mississippi Rifles (CULLUM-530); left Richmond for the Manassas field on 21 Jul 1861 (JONES-I-1861-07-21). Chesnut records a general ordering him off a spot under enemy guns, and Davis staying (CHESNUT-1905-p202) [single source, date not given].
- experience high: West Point 1828, Dragoon officer, colonel in Mexico, Senator, Secretary of War 1853-57 (CULLUM-530, BDAC-davis_jefferson, HEIT1-davis_jefferson).
- reputation_1861 high: elected President by the Provisional Congress (BDAC-davis_jefferson); Chesnut on 19 Feb 1861 says everybody wanted him as General-in-Chief or President (CHESNUT-1905-p6); Jones expected him unopposed in Nov 1861 (JONES-I-1861-10-29).
- micromanages: on 13 Nov 1862 Davis rebuked Randolph for ordering Holmes across the Mississippi without his sanction (JONES-I-1862-11-13); Jones says Randolph's latitude "became less and less" (JONES-I-1862-11-17) [clerk's view].
- holds_grudges: Chesnut reports the belief that Davis would "cripple the army" rather than let Beauregard have a showing (CHESNUT-1905-p100) and in Oct 1863 that he "detests" Johnston (CHESNUT-1905-p248); Beauregard says Davis's hostility after Manassas was "watchful and adroit" (BL-1-p224) [rival memoir: Beauregard].
- slaveholder: planter at Brierfield in Warren County, 1835-46 (BDAC-davis_jefferson, CULLUM-530); Jones notes a slave "used by the President" to carry papers, arrested Nov 1862 (JONES-I-1862-11-14). Number held: needs-source (1860 slave schedule).
- makes_policy: his proclamation on alien enemies of 16 Aug 1861 (JONES-I-1861-10-01); his proclamation of 23 Dec 1862 declaring Butler an outlaw and officers serving with Black troops to be held for execution (JONES-I-1862-12-23) [clerk's view]; OR II vol 5 pp.795-97 text not read: needs-source.
- Slavery, in his words: the message of 29 Apr 1861 says the labor of African slaves "was and is indispensable" to the staple crops (OR-IV-1-p259).
- proposed tag: favours_friends. He made his nephew J. R. Davis a brigadier (JONES-I-1862-12-05) and kept Northrop, a friend since the dragoons (JONES-I-1861-10-15).

#### benjamin_judah_p: Judah P. Benjamin
- competence average [contested]: Davis (own memoir, via BL) says he valued his "lucidity of intellect" and capacity for labour (BL-1-p106, DAVIS-RF-1881-I-p241) [own memoir]; Rhett calls him a man of "great fertility of mind and resource" and Davis's factotum (BL-1-p105) [rival memoir: Rhett Jr.]. Against: a congressional committee put Roanoke Island's loss on "Huger and Judah P. Benjamin" (JONES-I-1862-04-12); Imboden says he ordered a Manassas board's opinion struck (BL-1-p239) [rival memoir: Imboden]; Kennon says he was better fitted for law than War (BL-2-p77).
- nerve unknown: no act under pressure read; Romney order (OR I vol 5) not available locally.
- experience above: Senator from Louisiana 1853 to Feb 1861, lawyer (BDAC-benjamin_judah_p); no executive office.
- reputation_1861 above: Davis says he had a "very high reputation as a lawyer" (BL-1-p106) [own memoir]; BDAC shows reelection to the Senate in 1859.
- quarrelsome: open quarrel with Beauregard over a report (JONES-I-1861-10-30, JONES-I-1861-11-02); Johnston said there was no hope while Benjamin was Secretary (JONES-I-1862-03-25).
- political_patron: Davis chose him from Senate acquaintance (BL-1-p106) and he served Davis's views (BL-1-p105).

#### walker_leroy_p: Leroy P. Walker
- competence average [contested]: Jones calls him "a man of capacity" with a great memory for detail (JONES-I-1861-07-20); Rhett says he lacked fitness and let Davis run the department (BL-1-p104) [rival memoir: Rhett Jr.]. His report of 27 Apr 1861 lists volunteer calls of tens of thousands (OR-IV-1-p247) [own report].
- nerve below: Jones fears "his nerves are too finely strung" (JONES-I-1861-07-20) [single source]; Rhett says he "wisely resigned" after Davis refused his larger arms and camp numbers (BL-1-p104).
- experience below: Jones says he was a lawyer "never in governmental employment" (JONES-I-1861-05-20); Davis says he was a distinguished member of the Alabama bar (BL-1-p106) [own memoir]. State legislative service not sourced.
- reputation_1861 average: recommended by Yancey and described by Davis as eminent among Alabama politicians (BL-1-p104, BL-1-p106).
- political_patron: Yancey recommended him (BL-1-p104). ambitious: Rhett calls him "Ambitious" (BL-1-p104) [rival memoir: Rhett Jr.].
- Sumter: on 11 Apr 1861 Walker wired Beauregard that he did not want a needless bombardment and, if Anderson refused terms, to "reduce the fort" as he judged best (BL-1-p75, quoting the telegram; Stephen D. Lee). Walker's order of 10 Apr: needs-source.

#### randolph_george_w: George W. Randolph
- competence unknown: Jones writes "a lawyer of modest pretensions" and "merely a clerk" (JONES-I-1862-03-28, JONES-I-1862-07-05), a judgement on a post Davis controlled; no act of administration read. Searched Jones, Wright, Chesnut.
- nerve above: ordered Holmes to cross the Mississippi without prior sanction (JONES-I-1862-11-13) and resigned when told he had no discretion (JONES-I-1862-11-17) [single source].
- experience average: captain at Bethel, then colonel, lawyer (JONES-I-1862-03-28); brigadier 12 Feb 1862 (WRIGHT-p70).
- reputation_1861 below: Jones says he had few acquaintances and "got no votes at all" when he stood for Congress (JONES-I-1862-03-28). First appears Feb-Mar 1862.
- insubordinate: the Holmes order of Nov 1862 drew a sharp rebuke from Davis (JONES-I-1862-11-13).
- Jones 1862-11-21 reports Randolph condemned Col. Baylor's order to kill Arizona Indians (JONES-I-1862-11-21).

#### seddon_james_a: James A. Seddon
- competence above [single source]: "an able man (purely a civilian)" (JONES-I-1862-11-19), an impression on appointment.
- nerve unknown: nothing read; 30 Nov 1862 instruction on captured Black soldiers (NOTE-confederate-side) needs-source.
